You can organize workflow logs to monitor individual workflows separately or bring logs from related workflows together.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003ePreviously, every workflow attached to a Transfer Family server sent its execution logs to that server’s CloudWatch log group, so you could not set a separate log destination for each workflow. When creating a workflow through the AWS Transfer Family console or API, you can now select a workflow-level log group that is separate from the server’s log group. Transfer Family delivers workflow logs only to the selected group, so no server logging role is required. Workflow logs retain their existing structured JSON format and remain queryable using Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights. You can also send logs from multiple workflows to a shared log group to create consolidated metrics and dashboards for tracking workflow execution. If you do not select a structured log destination, workflow logs continue using the server’s role-based logging configuration when configured. Existing workflows continue using their current logging configuration.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThis feature is available in all AWS Regions where Transfer Family managed workflows are offered. You can now retain visibility of the client\u0027s source IP for IP-based auditing, access controls, and compliance when you use your own NLB.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003ePreviously, an NLB replaced the client\u0027s source IP with its own private IP address, so your Transfer Family logs and events recorded the NLB\u0027s address instead of the client\u0027s source IP. Because the NLB\u0027s private IP was the address presented to your custom identity provider during authentication, you couldn\u0027t authorize users based on their true source IP. With this launch, you can enable source IP preservation on your SFTP server so that the client\u0027s source IP is preserved. The preserved source IP is recorded in your logs and events and presented to your custom identity provider during authentication. Transfer Family now supports ML- KEM (FIPS-203), a post-quantum algorithm standardized by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), for SFTP file transfers. Quantum-resistant public-key exchange helps protect transfers of data files that require long-term confidentiality against \u003cem\u003e\u0026ldquo;harvest now, decrypt later\u0026ldquo; \u003c/em\u003ethreats. In such scenarios, an adversary may be recording present day traffic for decrypting once cryptanalytically relevant quantum computers become available.\u003cbr /\u003e\n\u003cbr /\u003e\nAWS Transfer Family offers fully managed support for the transfer of files over SFTP, AS2, FTPS, FTP, and web browser-based transfers directly into and out of AWS storage services. With this launch, you can now use post-quantum (PQ) hybrid security policies that combine classical Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman with quantum-resistant ML-KEM key exchanges between your AWS Transfer Family SFTP endpoints and clients like OpenSSH, Putty, and JSch that support PQ algorithms. When using a PQ hybrid policy, your Transfer Family SFTP server preserves the standard connection options supported by most clients today, while leveraging the most secure PQ connection options with clients that support quantum-resistant key exchange.\u003cbr /\u003e\n\u003cbr /\u003e\nML-KEM quantum-resistant key exchange for SFTP file transfers is supported in all AWS Regions where \u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/transfer-service.html\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003eAWS Transfer Family is available\u003c/a\u003e. Older PQ key exchange methods which included ML-KEM\u0026rsquo;s pre-standardized version (Kyber), \u003ca href\u003d\"https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/post-quantum-hybrid-sftp-file-transfers-using-aws-transfer-family/\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003eintroduced in AWS Transfer in 2023\u003c/a\u003e, will be removed from existing policies and no longer be included in the new PQ policy. To learn more about using PQ security policies to enable quantum-resistant key exchange, visit our \u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.aws.amazon.com/transfer/latest/userguide/post-quantum-security-policies.html\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003edocumentation\u003c/a\u003e.\u003cbr /\u003e\n\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\n","postDateTime":"2025-05-21T10:00:00-07:00","headline":"AWS Transfer Family announces ML-KEM quantum-resistant key exchange for SFTP"}},"tags":[{"id":"whats-new-v2#marketing-marchitecture#migration","locale":"en_US","tagNamespaceId":"whats-new-v2#marketing-marchitecture","name":"migration","description":"marketing:marchitecture/migration","dateCreated":"2023-11-08T19:09:35+0000","dateUpdated":"2023-11-08T19:09:35+0000"},{"id":"whats-new-v2#general-products#aws-govcloud-us","locale":"en_US","tagNamespaceId":"whats-new-v2#general-products","name":"aws-govcloud-us","description":"general:products/aws-govcloud-us","dateCreated":"2023-11-07T18:41:18+0000","dateUpdated":"2023-11-07T18:41:18+0000"},{"id":"whats-new-v2#year#2025","locale":"en_US","tagNamespaceId":"whats-new-v2#year","name":"2025","description":"\u003cp\u003e2025\u003c/p\u003e\n","dateCreated":"2024-12-30T16:07:22+0000","dateUpdated":"2024-12-30T16:07:22+0000"},{"id":"whats-new-v2#general-products#aws-transfer-family","locale":"en_US","tagNamespaceId":"whats-new-v2#general-products","name":"aws-transfer-family","description":"general:products/aws-transfer-family","dateCreated":"2023-11-09T13:52:49+0000","dateUpdated":"2023-11-09T13:52:49+0000"}]},{"item":{"id":"whats-new-v2#v1735398801","locale":"en_US","directoryId":"whats-new-v2","name":"launch-iSQKpsqjyh14fEbSxFhCzW","dateCreated":"2025-04-22T17:01:23+0000","dateUpdated":"2025-04-22T17:21:58+0000","additionalFields":{"nofollow":"0","noindex":"0","postBody":"\u003cp\u003eAWS Transfer Family introduces a Terraform module for deploying managed file transfer (MFT) server endpoints backed by Amazon S3. These new features enable customers to support a broader set of clients without making any client-side changes.\u003cbr /\u003e\r\n\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://aws.amazon.com/aws-transfer-family/\" target\u003d\"_blank\" rel\u003d\"noopener\"\u003eAWS Transfer Family\u003c/a\u003e provides fully managed file transfers over SFTP, FTPS, and FTP for Amazon S3 and Amazon EFS. When uploading files, file transfer clients can issue the SETSTAT command to change attributes of remote files, however, the SETSTAT command is not compatible with object storage systems, resulting in errors when these clients upload files to Amazon S3. With this launch, customers can now configure their Transfer Family servers to ignore the SETSTAT command so files are uploaded without SETSTAT triggering any errors. Customers who want to use SETSTAT to preserve timestamp of the original file or modify other file attributes should use Amazon EFS as a backend storage with Transfer Family.\u003cbr\u003e\r\n\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\u003cp\u003e Additionally, customers using FTPS Transfer Family servers can choose whether they want to process TLS session resumption requests. TLS session resumption can help to improve security and enhance performance by allowing clients to reuse recently negotiated TLS connections. However, some clients do not support TLS session resumption, and as a result, cannot establish connections to FTPS servers that enforce it. Now, customers have the option to enable, disable, or enforce TLS session resumption on their FTPS servers, helping customers expand client compatibility options.\u003cbr\u003e\r\n\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\u003cp\u003eSupport for these features is available in all AWS Regions where AWS Transfer Family is available. href\u003d\"https://aws.amazon.com/sftp/\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003eAWS Transfer Family\u003c/a\u003e now supports file transfers for Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) file systems, enabling AWS Transfer Family customers to easily and securely provide their business partners and end customers access to files stored in Amazon EFS file systems for use cases such as data distribution, supply chain workflows, content management, and web serving applications. With this launch, you can use AWS Transfer Family and Amazon EFS to migrate your file transfer workflows that rely on file storage without impacting your end users or application integrations, while eliminating the need to manage any file transfer infrastructure.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAWS Transfer Family has provided fully managed support for serverless file transfers to and from Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) since the service launched in 2018. With today\u0027s launch, AWS Transfer Family now also supports Amazon EFS enabling you to simplify and accelerate migration of file transfer workflows to AWS. Amazon EFS provides a simple, scalable, fully managed elastic file system for use with AWS services and on-premises resources. It is built to scale on demand to petabytes without disrupting applications, growing and shrinking automatically as you add and remove files, eliminating the need to provision and manage capacity to accommodate growth. When you select Amazon EFS as the data store for your AWS Transfer Family server, files transferred over Secure File Transfer Protocol (SFTP), File Transfer Protocol (FTP) and FTP over SSL (FTPS) will be stored in your Amazon EFS file systems. The transferred files are readily available to your business-critical applications running on Amazon EC2, as well as to containerized and serverless applications run using AWS services such as Amazon ECS, Amazon EKS, AWS Fargate, and AWS Lambda.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThis feature is available in all \u003ca href\u003d\"https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/global-infrastructure/regional-product-services/\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003eAWS Regions where AWS Transfer Family is available\u003c/a\u003e. Your existing users can continue to operate with their existing SFTP clients or applications.
